Cosmic Large-Scale Structure Formation from Newtonian Particle Dynamics
Tristan Daus, Elena Kozlikin

Abstract
We present results for the cosmic non-linear density-fluctuation power spectrum based on the analytical formalism developed in [ 1 ] which allows us to study cosmic structure formation based on Newtonian particle dynamics in phase-space. This framework provides a field-theory approach to a perturbative solution of the BBGKY-hierarchy where the resulting loop-expansion of the theory introduces a natural truncation criterion. We show that we are able to reproduce structure growth on large scales k ≤ 0.2 ​ h ​ Mpc − 1 k\leq 0.2\mathrm{h}\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1} to very high precision while on small and intermediate scales we find deviations of the order of 10 % 10\% from current numerical simulations. The results strongly suggest that a significant improvement may be achieved by restructuring the perturbation theory.



I
Introduction

The large-scale structure of the Universe contains an incredible wealth of information. At high redshift, temperature fluctuations in the cosmic microwave background (CMB) allow us to constrain the energy and matter content of the Universe and provide important evidence for the existence of dark matter and dark energy. Further constraints on their properties can be inferred by studying the evolution of those initial fluctuations. The current generation of large-scale surveys such as Euclid, LSST and SDSS
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
already provide data of an exquisite quality which can only be fully leveraged if our description of cosmic structure formation is sufficiently well understood. So far, numerical
N
-body simulations still provide the most accurate results for cosmic structure formation. The simple idea of propagating particles according to Newtonian dynamics – supplemented by sophisticated algorithms to reduce computational costs – appears to reproduce the best picture of the evolution of cosmic structure. However,
N
-body simulations come with a high computational cost and inevitable problems of shot noise, limited resolution, sample variance, and incomplete coverage of the relevant range of scales.
Without analytical approaches which complement numerical simulations, it will not be possible to reliably analyse large portions of the available data from current and especially from upcoming surveys. In addition, numerical simulations offer only limited insight into the underlying physical processes governing cosmic structure formation. Questions, for instance, about the universality of density profiles of dark matter haloes or the highly non-linear dynamics that leads to the formation of gravitationally bound objects, require a fundamental understanding of cosmic structure formation.
To provide such a framework which allows us to study the fundamental principles governing structure formation for in- and out-of-equilibrium systems is the main goal of the analytical approach presented in
[
1
]
. It describes the evolution of a classical
N
-particles ensemble based on the path-integral formulation of classical mechanics. In contrast to the available analytical approaches based on fluid dynamics, such as SPT, EFT or EFTofLSS
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
, the path-integral formulation of
[
1
]
is based on particle dynamics. Just as in numerical
N
-body simulations, the ability to describe structure formation even on small scales is based on the fact that we follow the phase-space evolution of the particle ensemble in time. Starting from Liouvilles’s equation we have, therefore, rigorously derived a microscopic perturbation theory (PT) which corresponds to an iterative solution of the well-known BBGKY-hierarchy based on the path-integral formulation of classical mechanics. Drawing on the formal resemblance to many-body quantum mechanics, we were able to apply a slightly modified version of the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation (HST) which leads to an effective field-theory description of the microscopic physics in terms of macroscopic fields. In
[
1
]
we have shown that this field theory presents a novel resummation scheme of microscopic processes. The perturbative loop-expansion of the macroscopic field theory in terms of propagators and vertices contains an infinite number of microscopic interactions already at lowest order. It resums microscopic processes which appear in arbitrary high orders of the microscopic PT. Viewed on a microscopic level, the loop-expansion provides a natural closure relation for the BBGKY-hierarchy of equations.

The approach presented in
[
1
]
generalises and extends concepts of Kinetic Field Theory (KFT)
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
. The key idea of describing the temporal evolution of an out-of-equilibrium
N
-particle ensemble using the path-integral formulation of classical mechanics underlies both formulations. The formulation of
[
1
]
in configuration space – instead of the
k
-space formulation of KFT – allows us to easily draw clear connections to established concepts of statistical physics and quantum many-body systems. Derivations presented in
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
could thus be considerably simplified and put into context of established methods as we show in
[
1
]
and the present work. While KFT is well-adapted for spatially homogeneous systems, the formulation of
[
1
]
presents a framework which is well suited to treat homogeneous as well as inhomogeneous systems in and out of equilibrium. This allows us to not only simplify but also extend the works of
[
14
]
and
[
15
]
to inhomogeneous systems.

The connections drawn in
[
1
]
to the BBGKY-hierarchy and the Vlasov equation highlight the advantages of a particle-based approach in comparison to fluid-based approaches such as SPT. The
stosszahlansatz
and the single-stream approximation on which Eulerian SPT is built, cause a permanent loss of information about microscopic degrees of freedom. Such a loss of microscopic information does not occur in the formalism of
[
1
]
or in KFT
[
18
]
since microscopic processes are included order by order in the perturbative expansion. The particle-based approach
[
1
]
is, therefore, valid on all scales.

In this work we present results for the cosmic density-fluctuation power spectrum for the tree-level and one-loop order in the field-theory framework of
[
1
]
. Before presenting our results, however, we first introduce the notation and conventions for this paper in Sec.
II
and familiarise the reader with the theoretical framework in Sec.
III
. We then specify the framework to the cosmological application in Sec.
IV
and derive the dynamics and initial conditions for our cosmological system in Sec.
V
.
Finally, in Sec.
VI
we present the tree-level and one-loop results for the cosmic density-fluctuation power spectrum and compare them to results from numerical simulations. As expected, we reproduce the tree-level result of
[
15
]
and present the first full result
1
1

1

In
[
19
]
a first result for the one-loop power spectrum was obtained using a perturbative expansion in the initial power spectrum.
for the one-loop power spectrum.

II
Notation and Conventions

II.1
N
-particle Phase Space

Given that we are working with an ensemble of
N
indistinguishable particles, it is important to differentiate between the
6N
-dimensional phase space describing all individual particle states and the 6-dimensional phase space representing averaged properties of the system as a whole. We therefore denote the coordinates of individual particles in their respective phase spaces by
sans serif
variables
\mathsf{x}=(\vec{\mathsf{q}},\vec{\mathsf{p}})
and use
x=(\vec{q},\vec{p})
for coordinates of the physical phase space.

Due to the indistinguishable nature of the particles, most quantities of interest, such as the Hamilton function, depend on the entire particle ensemble. To enhance readability, we therefore introduce a tensorial notation, bundling all objects with a particle index (e. g.
\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j}
) into multi-particle tensors,

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{j}(t)\otimes e_{j}\,,

(1)

where
e_{j}
is the
N
-dimensional canonical unit vector. The set of phase-space coordinates
\{\mathsf{x}_{j}\}
is now, for instance, represented by the tensor
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}
. Thus, the scalar product between two quantities
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{B}}
decomposes as

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A\cdot B}}=\sum_{j,i=1}^{N}(\mathsf{A}_{i}\otimes e_{i})\cdot(\mathsf{B}_{j}\otimes e_{j})=\sum_{j,i=1}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i}\mathsf{B}_{j}\underbrace{e_{i}\cdot e_{j}}_{=\delta_{ij}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{A}_{i}\mathsf{B}_{i}\,.

(2)

Functions depending on all particle positions can be compactly written as
F(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)
, and the integration measure of the
N
-particle phase space becomes

\mathrm{d}^{6N}\mathsf{x}=\mathrm{d}^{3N}\mathsf{q}\,\mathrm{d}^{3N}\mathsf{p}\equiv\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}\,.

(3)

II.2
Fourier Transform

It will often be convenient to work with the Fourier representation of a function
g(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)
, where
\vec{q}
and
\vec{p}
represent the position and momentum variable in phase space. To keep the notation simple, we use the same symbol for both, the original function and its Fourier transform, relying on the context of the arguments to distinguish between them. We thus define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{q,p}[g(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)](\vec{k},\vec{l},t)=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{q}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}\,\,g(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\vec{q}\cdot\vec{k}-\mathrm{i}\vec{p}\cdot\vec{l}}\;,

(4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k,l}^{-1}[g(\vec{k},\vec{l},t)](\vec{q},\vec{p},t)=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{l}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,g(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\vec{k}\cdot\vec{q}+\mathrm{i}\vec{l}\cdot\vec{p}}\;,

(5)

where the subscript of
\mathcal{F}_{q,p}
indicates over which variables the Fourier transform is performed.

II.3
Reduced Phase-Space Densities

The Liouville phase-space density contains the statistical properties of the system and is denoted by
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)
. The
s
-particle reduced phase-space density is defined as

f_{s}(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},\cdots,\vec{{q}}_{s},\vec{{p}}_{s},t)=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}\!\!\!\sum_{i_{1}\neq\cdots\neq i_{s}=1}^{N}\!\!\!\!\!\!\delta_{\mathrm{D}}\left(x_{1}-\mathsf{x}_{i_{1}}\right)\cdots\delta_{\mathrm{D}}\left(x_{s}-\mathsf{x}_{i_{s}}\right)\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}},t)\,,

(6)

and contains the statistical properties of an
s
-particle subset. Furthermore, we make use of the usual decomposition of
f_{s}
into a reducible and an irreducible part,

\displaystyle f_{2}({x}_{1},{x}_{2},t)=

\displaystyle f_{1}({x}_{1},t)f_{1}({x}_{2},t)+g_{2}({x}_{1},{x}_{2},t)\,,

(7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{3}({x}_{1},{x}_{2},{x}_{3},t)=&f_{1}({x}_{1},t)f_{1}({x}_{2},t)f_{1}({x}_{3},t)+f_{1}({x}_{1},t)g_{2}({x}_{2},{x}_{3},t)+f_{1}({x}_{2},t)g_{2}({x}_{1},{x}_{3},t)\\
&+f_{1}({x}_{3},t)g_{2}({x}_{1},{x}_{2},t)+g_{3}({x}_{1},{x}_{2},{x}_{3},t)\,,\end{split}

(8)

where
g_{s}({x}_{1},\cdots,{x}_{s},t)
are the
irreducible
reduced phase-space densities which describe by how much the system deviates from an uncorrelated state.

II.4
Field Theory Notation

For convenience, we introduce the short-hand notation for the four-dimensional object
X\coloneqq(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)
and its Fourier conjugate
S\coloneqq(\vec{k},\vec{l},t)
. In addition, we introduce the compact notation,

\int_{X}\coloneqq\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{q}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

(9)

III
Time Evolution of Classical Ensembles via Path Integrals

We consider a statistical ensemble of
N
particles of equal mass
m
in
6N
-dimensional phase space, with coordinates
\mathsf{x}_{j}=(\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j},\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{j})\,,\,\,\,j=1,\cdots,N
, where
\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j}
and
\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{j}
refer to the respective particle positions and momenta. At any given time
t
, the microstate of the system is thus described by the set
\{\mathsf{x}_{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{N}
, i. e. the collection of classical trajectories evaluated at time
t
. These are determined by the Hamilton function of the system,

H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}},t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}^{\,2}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}v(|\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j}|,t)\,,

(10)

which describes a system of particles mutually interacting through the homogeneous, possibly time-dependent, pair potential
v(|\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j}|,t)
.
The classical trajectories are subject to the Hamiltonian equations of motion,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\vec{\mathsf{q}}}_{i}\\
\dot{\vec{\mathsf{p}}}_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}},t)\\
-\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}}H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}},t)\end{pmatrix}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\dot{\mathsf{x}}_{i}=\omega\cdot\nabla_{i}H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}},t)\,,

(11)

where on the right hand side we used the symplectic structure
\omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{1}_{3\times 3}\\
-\mathbb{1}_{3\times 3}&0\end{pmatrix}
and the gradient
\nabla_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}}\,,\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}
acting on phase space. Using the tensorial notation introduced earlier, the equations of motion for the
N
-particle system can be expressed in the compact form,

0=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\mathcal{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}})=\dot{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\omega}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\cdot}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\nabla}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}}H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)\,,

(12)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\omega}}=\omega\otimes\mathbb{1}_{N\times N}
is the tensorial generalization of the symplectic structure and
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\nabla}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}}=(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\nabla}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\nabla}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})
generalizes the gradient to the
6N
-dimensional phase space. Within this setting, the current probabilistic state of the system is described by the Liouville phase-space density
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)
, whose time evolution is famously governed by Liouville’s equation

\frac{\partial\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)}{\partial t}=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}{m}\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}}\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{N}\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}}v(|\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j}|,t)\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)\equiv-\mathrm{i}\hat{L}\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)\,,

(13)

from which all desired quantities can be obtained. The typical procedure is to reduce (
13
) to the BBGKY-hierachy and to study the time evolution of those reduced phase-space distribution function. The usual approach in order to solve these evolution equations is to truncate the infinite BBGKY-hierarchy of equations which leads to the Boltzmann equation, or the Vlasov equation if collisions are neglected. These are then used to derive the hydrodynamical equations which form the basis for SPT and similar approaches.
Throughout this paper, however, we follow a different approach presented in
[
1
]
. It provides an iterative solution for the BBGKY-hierarchy and thereby avoids the need for an explicit truncation. Instead, the order of PT implicitly determines a truncation criterion for the BBGKY-hierarchy. In this way the full microscopic information on the system is retained and can be recovered order by order in PT. In the following sections we will summarise the main ideas and derivations of
[
1
]
and refer the reader to that publication for more details.

III.1
Path Integral Construction of Classical Time Evolution

The form of the Liouville equation (
13
) suggests that it may be treated in a similar way as the Schrödinger equation in a quantum mechanical context. This idea leads to the Koopman von Neumann formulation of classical mechanics – a framework that has been extensively studied in the literature
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
. Inspired by those striking similarities to many-body quantum systems, we follow
[
23
,
24
,
25
]
and set up a path-integral description in order to study the time evolution of the above system. Let us first note, that the Liouville equation (
13
) admits a formal solution in terms of a time ordered exponential
[
26
,
23
]
, given by

\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)=\hat{\mathcal{T}}\exp\left[-\mathrm{i}\int_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\hat{L}(t^{\prime})\right]\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t,t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,.

(14)

We can thus follow similar steps as in quantum mechanics to construct a path-integral representation of the above time evolution operator
\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t,t^{(\mathrm{i})})
in order to propagate the initial phase-space density. To keep things short, we outline the main ideas of the derivation and refer the reader to
[
1
]
for further information.

First note, that once an appropriate set of initial coordinates
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t=t^{(i)})
for all the particles has been chosen, their respective trajectories are uniquely defined by the classical equations of motion (
11
). Thus, by drawing realizations of initial coordinates from
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
and evolving them according to (
11
), the system evolves into a final phase-space density distribution
\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})})
. At any time
t>t^{(\mathrm{i})}
, the probability density of the system is given by,

\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t)=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

(15)

where the transition probability
K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
evolves the initial phase-space density of the system to the final one at time
t
. It reads

K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\,\,\delta_{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\,,

(16)

which ensures that at any time the particles are found on their respective classical trajectories.

The path integral representation of
K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}},t|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
is now constructed by discretizing the time evolution into
N
steps. The final result is then

K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)\,\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})})]\,,

(17)

where the functional integration measure
\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})})]
gives weight
0
to all trajectories but the classical one, with fixed end points at
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}
. We can further include the equations of motion in the form (
12
), with the help of a variable transformation in the functional measure, to obtain

\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})})]=\delta_{D}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\mathcal{E}}}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)]]\cdot\mathcal{J}\,.

(18)

As has been shown in
[
1
]
, we can absorb the constant Jacobian
\mathcal{J}
into a normalization constant. In a final step, we replace the remaining functional Dirac delta distribution with its corresponding Fourier transformed expression,

K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}t\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\cdot}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\omega\cdot\nabla}}H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),t)\right)\Bigg]\,,

(19)

where we introduced a doublet
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}=(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi_{q}}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi_{p}}})
conjugate to
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}=(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. The functional integration is carried out over all trajectories
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)
with fixed endpoints and all
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)
trajectories without endpoint restrictions. We may thus write the transition amplitude as

K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})},t^{(\mathrm{f})}|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]\Bigg]\,,

(20)

where we defined the action

\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]=\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{t^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathrm{d}t\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\cdot}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\omega\cdot\nabla}}H(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),t)\right)\,.

(21)

Upon inserting the Hamiltonian (
10
), the action can be split into a free and an interacting part

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]=\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]+\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]\,,

(22)

respectively given by

\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]=\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big\{\vec{\chi}_{\mathsf{q}_{i}}(t)\cdot\left(\dot{\vec{\mathsf{q}}}_{i}-\frac{\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}{m}\right)+\vec{\chi}_{\mathsf{p}_{i}}\cdot\dot{\vec{\mathsf{p}}}_{i}\Big\}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]=\int\limits_{t^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{N}\vec{\chi}_{\mathsf{p}_{i}}\cdot\nabla_{\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}}v(|\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{j}|,t)\,.

(23)

As we show in the following, the above representation of the classical transition amplitude enables the application of path-integral methods in order to analytically approach the above
N
-body problem.

III.2
Expectation Values and Free Phase-Space Density Cumulants

Our objects of interest are expectation values of observables
A(t)\equiv A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t))
that depend on the collective particle phase-space postions at time
t
. We define the time-ordered expectation value as

\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}A_{1}(t_{1})\cdots A_{k}(t_{k})\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)\,A_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t_{1}))\cdots A_{k}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t_{k}))\,\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]\Bigg]\,,

(24)

where
\hat{\mathcal{T}}
is the usual time-ordering operator. It is straightforward to verify
[
1
]
that the above path integral can be split at the respective operator insertions,

\begin{split}\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}A_{1}(t_{1})\cdots A_{k}(t_{k})\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{k}&\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}A_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1})K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1},t_{1}|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{2},t_{2})\cdots A_{k}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{k})K(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{k},t_{k}|\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,\end{split}

(25)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{s}=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t_{s})
and we assumed w. l. o. g. the time ordering
t^{(\mathrm{f})}>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{k}>t^{(\mathrm{i})}
. Using (
16
), the integrals in the above expression can be solved to give

\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}A_{1}(t_{1})\cdots A_{k}(t_{k})\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}\,A_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t_{1};\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\cdots A_{k}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t_{k};\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}))\,\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,,

(26)

which corresponds to the usual definition of unequal-time expectation values from statistical physics.

Of particular interest to us is the Klimontovich phase-space density, defined by

f(\vec{q},\vec{p},t):=f(\vec{q},\vec{p};\boldsymbol{\mathsf{q}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}(t))=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}(\vec{q}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}(t))\delta_{D}(\vec{p}-\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}(t))\,,

(27)

which counts the number of particles at a given position
x=(\vec{q},\vec{p})
of the
6
-dimensional physical phase space
2
2

2

Notice that we denote the coordinates of the 6-dimensional physical phase space by
x=(\vec{q},\vec{p})
while we use the
sans serif
version
\mathsf{x}=(\vec{\mathsf{q}},\vec{\mathsf{p}})
to denote particle positions in
6N
-dimensional phase-space.
. The Klimontovich phase-space density can be used to obtain further statistical quantities. Especially relevant in the cosmological context are the moments of the momentum distribution, which can generally be written as

\mathcal{O}(\vec{q},t)=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}\,\,F_{\mathcal{O}}(\vec{p})\,f(\vec{q},\vec{p}t)\,,

(28)

with some appropriate function
F_{\mathcal{O}}(\vec{p})
. For instance, we find for the particle number density
\rho(\vec{q},t)
or the momentum density
\Pi(\vec{q},t)
the expressions

\rho(\vec{q},t)=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}p\,f(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}(\vec{q}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}(t))\,,\quad\Pi(\vec{q},t)=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}p\,\vec{p}\,f(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\vec{\mathsf{p}}_{i}(t)\,\delta_{D}(\vec{q}-\vec{\mathsf{q}}_{i}(t))\,.

(29)

We may, therefore, relate an arbitrary
k
-point correlator of the above objects to the respective
k
-point correlation function of the Klimontovich phase-space density,

\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{O}_{1}(\vec{q}_{1},t_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{k}(\vec{q}_{k},t_{k})\right\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}_{k}\,\,F_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}(\vec{p}_{1})\cdots F_{\mathcal{O}_{k}}(\vec{p}_{k})\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1},t_{1})\cdots f(\vec{q}_{k},\vec{p}_{k},t_{k})\right\rangle\,.

(30)

As a central element of our discussion, we therefore define the
k
-point phase-space density correlation function

C_{f\cdots f}(x_{1},t_{1},\cdots x_{k},t_{k}):=\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(x_{1},t_{1})\cdots f(x_{k},t_{k})\right\rangle\,,

(31)

and the associated cumulant

G_{f\cdots f}(x_{1},t_{1},\cdots x_{k},t_{k}):=\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(x_{1},t_{1})\cdots f(x_{k},t_{k})\right\rangle_{c}\,,

(32)

where the subscript
\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{c}
refers to the connected part of the respective correlator. For instance, we find

G_{ff}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})=C_{ff}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})-C_{f}(x_{1},t_{1})C_{f}(x_{2},t_{2})\,,

(33)

as usual.

In the absence of interactions, expressions of the form (
24
) or (
26
) are exactly solvable. In that case, the free trajectory for a particle moving from
(x^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})
to
(x_{1},t_{1})
has an analytical solution, which is given by

{\mathsf{x}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;x^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})=(\vec{{q}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;x^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{{p}}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t;x^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})}))=\left(\vec{q}\,^{(\mathrm{i})}+\frac{\vec{p}\,^{(\mathrm{i})}}{m}(t-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{p}\,^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,.

(34)

The integrals can thus be solved and for the free one- and two-point
f
-cumulants one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{f}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1})=&\,f_{1}^{(0)}(\vec{q_{1}},\vec{p}_{1},t_{1})\,,\end{split}

(35)

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{ff}^{(0)}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})=&\,\delta_{\mathrm{D}}\left(x_{1}-x_{\text{cl}}(t_{1};x_{2},t_{2})\right)\,f_{1}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1},t_{1})+g_{2}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1},t_{1},\vec{q}_{2},\vec{p}_{2},t_{2})\,,\end{split}

(36)

with the freely evolved one- and two-particle reduced phase space densities

\displaystyle f_{1}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{1},\vec{p}_{1},t_{1})\equiv

\displaystyle\,f_{1}\left(\vec{q}_{1}-\frac{\vec{p}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{p}_{1},t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,,

(37)

\displaystyle g_{2}^{(0)}(\vec{q_{1}},\vec{p}_{1},t_{1},\vec{q_{2}},\vec{p}_{2},t_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle\,g_{2}\left(\vec{q}_{1}-\frac{\vec{p}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{p}_{1},\vec{q}_{2}-\frac{\vec{p}_{2}}{m}(t_{2}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{p}_{2},t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,.

(38)

f_{1}^{(0)}
and
g_{2}^{(0)}
generalize the reduced densities defined in (
6
) to the case of unequal times evolved by the free Liouville equation. They are obtained by shifting the respective initial reduced density by the free trajectory. Thus, we can relate all density cumulants, as expected, to the freely evolved initial phase space density. Note, that the difference between the two-point phase-space density cumulant
G_{ff}^{(0)}
and the true two-particle correlation
g_{2}
is given by the first term on the right-hand side of (
36
) and represents a one-particle contribution to
G_{ff}^{(0)}
coming from the diagonal part of the double sum. It is merely a consequence of the discreteness of the system and describes the possibility of picking the same particle twice, while measuring the correlation function. The Dirac delta distribution identifies the particle at
\vec{q}_{1}
with the particle freely moving from
\vec{q}_{2}
at time
t_{2}
. Such contributions always arise when dealing with discrete particle systems and are referred to as
Poisson shot-noise
effects. The general
r
-point
f
-cumulant therefore has the form

G^{(0)}_{f\cdots f}(x_{1},t_{1},\dots,x_{r},t_{r})=\sum_{\text{shot noise}}+\,\,g_{r}\left(\vec{q}_{1}-\frac{\vec{p}_{1}}{m}(t_{1}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{p}_{1},\dots\,,\vec{q}_{r}-\frac{\vec{p}_{r}}{m}(t_{r}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}),\vec{p}_{r},t^{(\mathrm{i})}\right)\,,

(39)

where the sum includes all lower-order particle contributions. Most importantly, (
39
) shows that the free density-cumulants can be expressed in terms of the initial reduced phase-space densities.

The exact computation of expectation values as in (
24
) is generally not possible due to the presence of an interaction potential. It is, therefore, inevitable to employ some sort of approximation procedure. The most straightforward approach is canonical perturbation theory, which consists of expanding the exponential of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
in (
24
) in a Taylor series and sorting the resulting terms by powers of the interaction potential. However, since it is not the subject of this work, we refer the interested reader to
[
1
]
for technical details and to
[
27
,
28
]
for similar cosmological applications. Instead, we will construct a perturbation theory based on a re-formulation of the theory in terms of macroscopic fields.

III.3
Construction of the Field Theory

Despite being the most straightforward approach, canonical perturbation theory is not very suitable for our purposes, due to the vast number of diagrams that have to be evaluated in order to obtain the desired accuracy (see
[
1
]
and
[
27
,
28
]
for a discussion). Therefore, in
[
1
]
we proposed a different approach based on the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation (HST), well-known from equilibrium statistical mechanics. It allows us to convert the particle-based description to a field theory based on macroscopic fields. We give a brief summary of the derivation leading to said field theory here and refer the interested reader to
[
1
]
for details.

We begin by introducing the so-called response field
\mathcal{B}(X)
, in addition to the Klimontovich phase-space density
f(X)
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(X)\equiv\mathcal{B}(\vec{{q}}_{1},\vec{{p}}_{1},t_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\vec{\chi}_{p_{i}}(t_{1})\cdot\nabla_{\vec{q}_{i}}v(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{{q}}_{1}|,t_{1})\,,

(40)

where
X=(\vec{q},\vec{p},t)
denotes the combined phase-space and time coordinates. The interaction part of the action (
22
) can then be written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]=\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\,\mathcal{B}(X_{1})\,\delta_{D}(X_{1}-X_{2})\,f(X_{2})\,,

(41)

with
\delta_{D}(X_{1}-X_{2})=\delta_{D}(\vec{{q}}_{1}-\vec{{q}}_{2})\delta_{D}(\vec{{p}}_{1}-\vec{{p}}_{2})\delta_{D}(t_{1}-t_{2})\equiv\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})
. In order to apply the HST, we bring the action (
41
) into the quadratic form,

\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t);J]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\Big(\Phi(X_{1})+{J}(X_{1})\Big)^{\top}\cdot\sigma(X_{1},X_{2})\cdot\Big(\Phi(X_{2})+{J}(X_{2})\Big)\,,

(42)

where we have collected the fields into a doublet
\Phi(X)=(\mathcal{B}(X),\,\,f(X))^{\top}
and introduced the sources
{J}(X)=(J_{f}(X),\,\,J_{\mathcal{B}}(X))^{\top}
for the
f
- and
B
-fields, as well as the involutory matrix

\sigma(X_{1},X_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})\\
\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(43)

The HST now consists of writing the exponential of the quadratic form as a Gaussian functional integral. Following
[
1
]
, we get

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}[J]=\mathcal{N}\int\mathcal{D}\Psi\,\exp\Bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\Psi^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\sigma^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\cdot\Psi(X_{2})+\int_{X_{1}}{J}^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\Psi(X_{1})\Bigg]\cdot\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}\big[\Psi\big]\,,\end{split}

(44)

with

\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}[\Psi]=\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}\int\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}\varrho_{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})},t^{(\mathrm{i})})\int\limits_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{i})}}^{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}^{(\mathrm{f})}}\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t)\mathcal{D}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)\exp\Bigg[\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}(t),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}(t)]+\int_{X_{1}}\Psi^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\Phi(X_{1})\Bigg]\,.

(45)

In the above, we have introduced the macroscopic-field doublet
\Psi(X)=(\Psi_{f}(X),\,\,\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X))
conjugate to
\Phi(X)
and used that
\sigma(X_{1},X_{2})=\sigma^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})
. Notice that the macroscopic fields
\Psi_{f}(X)
and
\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X)
are independent of microscopic degrees of freedom, but couple linearly to
\Phi(X)
in
\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}
. This makes
\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}
the generating functional of
free

f
-
\mathcal{B}
correlation functions, as one can easily verify by taking functional derivatives w. r. t.
\Psi_{f}
and
\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}
. The generating functional
\mathcal{Z}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}
, thus, contains the full microscopic statistics. Introducing the Schwinger functional
\mathcal{W}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}[\Psi]\equiv\ln\Big[\mathcal{Z}_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}[\Psi]\Big]
, we can bring (
44
) into the compact form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}[J]=\mathcal{N}\int\mathcal{D}\Psi\,\exp\Bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\Psi^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\sigma^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\cdot\Psi(X_{2})+\int_{X_{1}}{J}^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\Psi(X_{1})+\mathcal{W}^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}\big[\Psi\big]\Bigg]\,.

(46)

The Schwinger functional has the following series expansion,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}[\Psi]=\sum_{r,s=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r!s!}\Bigg[\prod_{m=1}^{r}\int_{X_{m}}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{m})\Bigg]\Bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{s}

\displaystyle\int_{X_{n}^{\prime}}\Psi_{f}(X_{n}^{\prime})\Bigg]\,G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\cdots X_{s}^{\prime})\,,

(47)

which introduces the free
f\mathcal{B}
-cumulants
G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
, containing
r
phase-space densities and
s
response fields,

G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\cdots X_{s}^{\prime})=\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(X_{1})\cdots f(X_{r})\mathcal{B}(X_{1}^{\prime})\cdots\mathcal{B}(X_{s}^{\prime})\right\rangle^{(0)}_{c}\,.

(48)

Note, that the mean has to be taken w. r. t. the
free
microscopic action as the potential is included in the
\mathcal{B}
field. The
G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
’s thus generalize the pure
f
-cumulants discussed in the end of Sec.
III.2
to include the response field. General expressions for the
f\mathcal{B}
-cumulants have been derived in
[
1
]
and we discuss their form and physical meaning in the next section. Furthermore, we provide expressions for the cumulants required in the course of this paper in Appendix
B
.

Together with (
46
), equation (
47
) defines the action of a statistical field theory where the generating functional has the form

\mathcal{Z}[J]=\mathcal{N}\int\mathcal{D}\Psi\exp\Big[-\mathcal{S}[\Psi]+\int_{X_{1}}{J}^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\Psi(X_{1})\Big]=\mathcal{N}\int\mathcal{D}\Psi\exp\Big[-\mathcal{S}_{0}[\Psi]-\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\Psi]+\int_{X_{1}}{J}^{\top}(X_{1})\cdot\Psi(X_{1})\Big]\,,

(49)

with the free macroscopic action containing all terms which are at most quadratic in the fields
3
3

3

Note that there exists no pure
\mathcal{B}
-field cumulant. See
[
1
]
or the following discussion for more details.
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\Psi_{f},\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}]=&\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1})\left[\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})-G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})\right]\Psi_{f}(X_{2})-\int_{X_{1}}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1})G^{(0)}_{f}(X_{1})\\
&-\frac{1}{2!}\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1})\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{2})G^{(0)}_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})\;,\end{split}

(50)

and the remaining terms defining the self-interactions of the fields,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}[\Psi]=

\displaystyle-\sum_{r+s>2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r!s!}\Bigg[\prod_{m=1}^{r}\int_{X_{m}}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{m})\Bigg]\Bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{s}\int_{X_{n}^{\prime}}\Psi_{f}(X_{n}^{\prime})\Bigg]\,G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\cdots X_{s}^{\prime})\,.

(51)

Introducing the inverse tree-level propagator

\Delta^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{1}-G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}\\
\mathbb{1}-G^{(0)}_{\mathcal{B}f}&G^{(0)}_{ff}\end{pmatrix}(X_{1},X_{2})\,,\quad\text{with}\quad G^{(0)}_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{1},X_{2})=G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{2},X_{1})

(52)

we can write the quadratic part of the action (
50
) as

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\Psi]=&\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{1},X_{2}}\Psi(X_{1})\cdot\Delta^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\cdot\Psi(X_{2})-\int_{X_{1}}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1})G^{(0)}_{f}(X_{1})\,.\end{split}

(53)

Note, that by construction we have

\left\langle f(X_{1})\cdots f(X_{k})\right\rangle=\frac{\delta^{k}}{\delta{J}_{f}(X_{1})\cdots\delta{J}_{f}(X_{k})}\mathcal{Z}[J]\Bigg\rvert_{{J}=0}\,,

(54)

where the above expectation value is exact as no approximations have been made so far. We may now apply the usual field theoretic methods in order to compute correlation functions.

First, we replace the fields
\Psi_{f}
and
\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}
in (
51
) by the functional derivative w. r. t. their respective source field and find the usual expression

\mathcal{Z}[J]=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\exp\bigg[\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{I}}\bigg[\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{f}},\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{\mathcal{B}}}\bigg]\bigg]\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{0}[J]\;,

(55)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{0}[J]
is given by the quadratic part of the action,
4
4

4

The normalization
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}
is chosen such that
\mathcal{Z}[0]=1=\mathcal{Z}_{0}[0]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}[J]=\mathcal{N}^{\prime\prime}\int\mathcal{D}\Psi_{f}\mathcal{D}\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}\exp\bigg[-\mathcal{S}_{0}[\Psi_{\mathcal{B}},\Psi_{f}]+\int_{X_{1}}J_{f}(X_{1})\Psi_{f}(X_{1})+\int_{X_{1}}J_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1})\Psi_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1})\bigg]\,.

(56)

The above path integration for the tree-level generating functional can be performed analytically (see
[
1
]
for details) and yields

\mathcal{Z}_{0}[J]=\exp\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}J\cdot\Delta\cdot J+J_{f}\cdot\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}\cdot G_{f}^{(0)}\Bigg]=\exp\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}J_{f}\\
J_{\mathcal{B}}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{ff}&\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}\\
\Delta_{\mathcal{B}f}&0\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}J_{f}\\
J_{\mathcal{B}}\end{pmatrix}+J_{f}\cdot\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}\cdot G_{f}^{(0)}\Bigg]\,,

(57)

which allows us to express the field theory in terms of the propagators

\displaystyle\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle=\Big[\mathbb{1}-G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}\Big]^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\,

(58)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{2},X_{1})\,

(59)

\displaystyle\Delta_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\bar{X}_{1},\bar{X}_{2}}\,\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{1})\,G^{(0)}_{ff}(\bar{X}_{1},\bar{X}_{2})\,\Delta_{\mathcal{B}f}(\bar{X}_{2},X_{2})\;,

(60)

and its vertices
G^{(0)}_{f\dots f\mathcal{B}\dots\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime})
.
We have, thus, developed a field theoretic formulation for the
N
-particle system in which the phase-space density plays the role of the fundamental degree of freedom, as opposed to the path integral in (
19
) which is constructed entirely on microscopic degrees of freedom. We will, therefore, refer to the HS transformed formulation as the
macroscopic
(field) theory.

III.4
Free Cumulants

As we have seen above, the free
f
-
\mathcal{B}
-cumulants play the role of fundamental building blocks of the field theory, as they appear in both, the propagators and the vertices. They contain information on the
microscopic
degrees of freedom. The exact derivation of free
f
-
\mathcal{B}
-cumulants is rather involved and has been presented in detail in
[
1
]
. Let us therefore only summarize the main results here focussing on their structure and physical properties.

The general
f
-
\mathcal{B}
-cumulant is defined as the connected correlation function

G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\cdots X_{s}^{\prime})=\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(X_{1})\cdots f(X_{r})\mathcal{B}(X_{1}^{\prime})\cdots\mathcal{B}(X_{s}^{\prime})\right\rangle^{(0)}_{c}\,.

(61)

The prescription of how this object is generally computed is given by equation (
25
) where the free propagator has to be inserted. The pure
f
-cumulants (
39
) have already been discussed at the end of section
III.2
and describe the free evolution of a connected
k
-point density cluster, propagating the initial correlations, if present. If one or more
\mathcal{B}
-fields are involved, however, the expressions become more complex. Note, that in order to derive (
25
), the
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\chi}}
-path integrals have been carried out. Since the response field (
40
) contains a
\vec{\chi}_{p_{i}}
, it is promoted to an operator,

\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{B}}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla_{\vec{q}_{i}}v(|\vec{q}_{i}-\vec{{q}}_{1}|,t_{1})\cdot\nabla_{\vec{p}_{i}}\,,

(62)

acting on all Liouville propagators and response fields on its right in (
25
). The time ordering in which the operators in (
25
) appear becomes relevant since the operators do not commute. Furthermore, if the operator associated with the latest time is a
\mathcal{B}
-field, the corresponding expression vanishes identically. This can easily be verified by performing a partial integration and requiring that the Liouville phase-space density vanishes at infinite momenta.
5
5

5

This is required for the Liouville phase-space density to be normalizable.
In particular, we find

G_{\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\dots\,,X_{s})=0\,,

(63)

and the rule

G_{f\dots f\mathcal{B}\dots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\dots X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\dots X_{s}^{\prime})=0\,,\quad\text{if}\,\,\,\,\exists\,\,\,\bar{s}\in[1,\dots\,,s]:t_{\bar{s}}\geq t_{\bar{r}}\,\,\forall\,\,\bar{r}\in[1,\dots\,,r]\,.

(64)

The above rule is simply a causality restriction. A given density cluster can only respond to interactions that happen at earlier times. Consider for instance the simplest mixed cumulant
G_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
that appears in the propagator
\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}
. It is defined as

G_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},X_{2})=\left\langle\hat{\mathcal{T}}f(X_{1})\mathcal{B}(X_{2})\right\rangle_{c}=\left\langle f(X_{1})\mathcal{B}(X_{2})\right\rangle_{c}\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})+\left\langle\mathcal{B}(X_{2})f(X_{1})\right\rangle_{c}\theta(t_{2}-t_{1})\,.

(65)

Following the discussion above, the second term on the right-hand side of (
65
) vanishes identically. The integration in the first term yields

G_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},X_{2})=\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{2\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.69pt{\vbox to1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{
{\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{}{}{}
{}{}{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.72 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.85555pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 6.72 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.41 3.21 C -2.21 2.01 0 0.2 0.6 0 C 0 -0.2 -2.21 -2.01 -2.41 -3.21}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}1}(t_{2};t^{(\mathrm{i})})\,G_{f}^{(0)}(X_{1})\,\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})\,,

(66)

with the interaction operator defined as

\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{b\parbox{5.69046pt}{\hbox to6.69pt{\vbox to1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.72 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.85555pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 6.72 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.41 3.21 C -2.21 2.01 0 0.2 0.6 0 C 0 -0.2 -2.21 -2.01 -2.41 -3.21}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}a}(t_{b};t^{(\mathrm{i})}):=\nabla_{\vec{q}_{a}}v\left(\Big\rvert\vec{q}_{a}-\frac{\vec{p}_{a}}{m}(t_{a}-t_{b})-\vec{q}_{b}\Big\rvert,t_{b}\right)\cdot\left[-\frac{t_{b}-t^{(\mathrm{i})}}{m}\nabla_{\vec{q}_{a}}+\bar{\nabla}_{\vec{p}_{a}}\right]\,,

(67)

where
\bar{\nabla}_{\vec{p}}
only acts on the explicit momentum argument of
G_{f}^{(0)}
. The form of
G_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
is closely related to the
G_{f}
-cumulant at first-order in canonical perturbation theory and corresponds to the time evolved Liouville operator. Thus, it describes how the free one-point cumulant evolving to position
\vec{q}_{1}
at time
t_{1}

responds
to a force exhibited at an
earlier
time
t_{2}
by a particle at position
\vec{q}_{2}
. This can be generalized to higher-order mixed cumulants as follows:
G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{s},X_{1^{\prime}},\cdots,X_{r^{\prime}})
describes the response of the freely evolved
s
-point cumulant to
r
interactions at times
t_{1^{\prime}},\cdots,t_{r^{\prime}}
with external particles located at
\vec{q}_{1^{\prime}},\cdots,\vec{q}_{r^{\prime}}
. The cumulant thus consists of a differential operator acting on the freely evolved
pure

f
-cumulant,

G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{s},X_{1^{\prime}},\cdots,X_{r^{\prime}})\simeq\hat{\mathcal{D}}\cdot G_{f\cdots f}^{(0)}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{s})\,.

(68)

Importantly, due to (
64
) the cumulant introduces a time flow from the external particles sourcing the interaction to the densities being deflected. This fact is incorporated via the Heaviside functions introduced by the time-ordering in the definition of (
32
). By inserting the general expression of pure
f
-cumulants (
39
), it can be immediately seen that the mixed
f\mathcal{B}
-cumulants can also be expressed in terms of initial reduced phase-space densities. This is an important property of the free cumulants which shows again that once the initial conditions and the particle trajectories are fixed, the evolution of the system is fully determined.

In
[
1
]
we provide a detailed discussion on the free cumulants, including the case of multiple response fields, which is beyond the scope of this paper. In Appendix
B
we present a list of the Fourier representation of all cumulants relevant for the results of the present work.

III.5
Propagators and Feynman Diagrams

We now turn to the interpretation of the propagator equations (
58
) and (
60
). We first note, that the inverse in (
58
) is to be understood in the functional sense, i. e. as the solution of the integral equation

\int_{\bar{X}_{1}}\Big[\mathbb{1}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{1})-G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{1})\Big]\cdot\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(\bar{X}_{1},X_{2})=\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})\,.

(69)

By making the ansatz

\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})=\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})+\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})\,,

(70)

we find that the corresponding integral equation for
\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}
is given by

\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})=G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})+\int_{\bar{X}}G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X})\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(\bar{X},X_{2})\,.

(71)

Due to the Heaviside function in (
66
) we also find
\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})\propto\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})
, which preserves the causal propagation. We therefore call
{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})
and
{\Delta}_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{1},X_{2})
the
retarded
and
advanced causal propagators
, respectively, and define the Feynman rules,

\displaystyle\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})

(72)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle=\hbox to47.95pt{\vbox to1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

(73)

where the arrow indicates the time flow from earlier to later times. Equation (
71
) is of Volterra-Fredholm type and has to be solved numerically in general. However, we will see shortly, how symmetry assumptions of the system under consideration can simplify the above integral equation. By iterating (
71
) we find the Neumann series representation of the causal propagator,

\displaystyle\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}(X_{1},X_{2})+G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})+\int_{\bar{X}_{1}}G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{1})\,G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(\bar{X}_{1},X_{2})+\cdots=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big[G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}\Big]^{n}(X_{1},X_{2})\,,

(74)

where the power of
n
on the right hand side implies integration. The interpretation of the causal propagators, therefore, becomes clear: each
G_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
describes how a one-point phase-space density responds to an incoming interaction, which is sourced by a phase-space density that has itself already been influenced by an earlier interaction. This process can repeat arbitrarily many times within the time interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]
. Figure
1
provides a graphical representation of this iteration.

[formula]

[formula]
[formula]

Figure 1
:
Graphical illustration of the processes contributing to the causal propagator
\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}
(
left panel
) and to the statistical propagator
\Delta_{ff}
(
right panel
). Phase-space densities sourcing the interactions are presented in gray. Dashed lines indicate the connected part of the correlation between these points.

Thus, objects involving
\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}
propagators are, by construction, non-perturbative in terms of the microscopic interaction potential as they involve an analytical or a numerical resummation of the interaction series (
74
). Note, that the potential does not depend on the particle momenta.
G_{f\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}(X_{1},X_{2})
is, therefore, independent of
\vec{p}_{2}
as well, which is inherited by
\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}
. We can, thus, perform the integration over the momenta which simplifies the integral equation in (
71
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})=

\displaystyle\,G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})+\int_{\vec{q}^{\,\prime},t^{\prime}}G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X})\widetilde{\Delta}_{\rho\mathcal{B}}(\vec{q}^{\,\prime},t^{\prime},\vec{q}_{2},t_{2})\,,

(75)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\rho\mathcal{B}}(\vec{q}_{1},t_{1},\vec{q}_{2},t_{2})=

\displaystyle\,G^{(0)}_{\rho\mathcal{B}}(\vec{q}_{1},t_{1},\vec{q}_{2},t_{2})+\int_{\vec{q}^{\,\prime},t^{\prime}}G^{(0)}_{\rho\mathcal{B}}(\vec{q}_{1},t_{1},\vec{q}^{\,\prime},t^{\prime})\widetilde{\Delta}_{\rho\mathcal{B}}(\vec{q}^{\,\prime},t^{\prime},\vec{q}_{2},t_{2})\,.

(76)

This reduces the dimensionality of the integral equation from seven to four, since interactions are only sourced by the particle density
6
6

6

In principle, we could have performed the whole derivation only using the particle density instead of the full phase space density. However, this would loose the momentum information which is now restored in the final iteration in the above equation.

\rho(\vec{q},t)
. Therefore, all momentum integrals are trivially performed during the iteration procedure, apart from the final one which restores the information on the momentum distribution. Nevertheless, the solution of the system (
75
) is rather involved and requires numerical discretization in time and space. However, in the next sections we will see how symmetry considerations will simplify the above system.

Similarly, we find an interpretation of the propagator (
60
): Two initially correlated phase-space densities, described by
G^{(0)}_{ff}
, source the interaction chain included in the
\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}
propagators which we described above. The two external densities at
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
thus remain fully connected, see Figure
1
. Since
\Delta_{ff}
thus carries statistical information, we refer to it as the
statistical propagator
and equip it with the Feynman rule

\Delta_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})=\int_{\bar{X}_{1},\bar{X}_{2}}\,\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{1})\,G^{(0)}_{ff}(\bar{X}_{1},\bar{X}_{2})\,\Delta_{\mathcal{B}f}(\bar{X}_{2},X_{2})=\;\hbox to47.95pt{\vbox to2.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-1.325pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

), equation (

) can be expanded such that

\begin{split}\Delta_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})=G^{(0)}_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})+&\int_{\bar{X}_{1}}\widetilde{\Delta}_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{1})G^{(0)}_{ff}(\bar{X}_{1},X_{2})+\int_{\bar{X}_{2}}G^{(0)}_{ff}(X_{1},\bar{X}_{2})\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathcal{B}f}(\bar{X}_{2},X_{2})\\

Since there is no pure
G_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
-cumulant, we also have
\Delta_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}=0
.

Last but not least, the vertices
G_{f\cdots f\mathcal{B}\cdots\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
describe how
r
interactions affect the future evolution of a connected
s
-particle cluster. We therefore represent vertices by the following Feynman rule,

G^{(0)}_{f\dots f\mathcal{B}\dots\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{r},X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{s}^{\prime})=\;\hbox to61.79pt{\vbox to50.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.89346pt\lower-25.46024pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(79)

where we represent the time flow by arrows attached to the amputated external legs. The solid line will be attached to incoming interaction chains, while the dashed lines will source outgoing interactions, sourced by the cumulant itself. Importantly, due to the rule in (
64
) there has to be at least one outgoing line, representing the density that is being deflected, meaning that there is no sink in the time flow. However, a pure phase-space density cumulant
G_{f\cdots f}^{(0)}
will only source outgoing interaction chains. We can now understand the general construction of a Feynman diagram. All components of the diagram, i. e. propagators and vertices, are attached to each other such that the densities of one component act as sources for the response functions of another. This ensures a continuous time flow, as indicated by the arrows, which can only terminate at the external phase-space densities. Within vertices, the time flow can split or merge, indicating how a single density influences the future evolution of multiple densities or is affected through interactions with multiple densities from the past.

III.6
Macroscopic Self-Energy

With the generating functional (
49
) at hand, we now wish to construct a systematic way in order to compute full correlation functions. As we mainly aim at the computation of cumulants, i. e. connected correlation functions, we define the Schwinger functional
\mathcal{W}[J]
as

\mathcal{W}[J]:=\ln\Big[\mathcal{Z}[J]\Big]

(80)

which generates the connected correlation functions upon taking functional derivatives w. r. t. the source field
J
,

\frac{\delta^{r}}{\delta J(X_{1})\cdots\delta J(X_{r})}\mathcal{W}[J]=G(X_{1},\cdots X_{r})

(81)

where
G
denotes the full cumulant.
7
7

7

In the above equation we have suppressed the tensor indices standing for
f
and
\mathcal{B}
.
G
is therefore a tensorial object.
In particular, the full propagator of the theory is given by

\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta J(X_{1})\delta J(X_{2})}\mathcal{W}[J]=G(X_{1},X_{2})\,.

(82)

As ususal, we introduce the statistical effective action
\Gamma[\Psi]
as the functional Legendre transform of the Schwinger functional,

\Gamma[\Psi]=\int_{X_{1}}J(X_{1})\cdot\Psi(X_{1})-\mathcal{W}[J]\,,\quad\text{with}\quad\Psi(X)=\frac{\delta\mathcal{W}[J]}{\delta J(X)}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\delta\Gamma[\Psi]}{\delta\Psi(X)}=J(X)\,.

(83)

\Gamma[\Psi]
will help us relate the full, unknown propagator
G
to the bare, known propagator
\Delta
. As in the QFT analogue, the effective action
\Gamma[\Psi]
generalizes the bare action
\mathcal{S}[\Psi]
in (
49
) to the full statistical theory, in which all vertices already contain the full statistical information. Using the functional relations in (
83
), one finds

\int_{X_{2}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{W}[J]}{\delta J(X_{1})\delta J(X_{2})}\cdot\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma[\Psi]}{\delta\Psi(X_{2})\delta\Psi(X_{3})}=\delta_{\mathrm{D}}\left(X_{1}-X_{3}\right)\,,

(84)

which implies

\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma[\Psi]}{\delta\Psi(X_{1})\delta\Psi(X_{2})}=G^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\,,

(85)

i. e. giving the full inverse propagator in contrast to

\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\Psi]}{\delta\Psi(X_{1})\delta\Psi(X_{2})}=\Delta^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\,,

(86)

which follows form (
53
) and only contains tree-level information. We therefore define the macroscopic
self-energy

\Sigma(X_{1},X_{2})
as the difference

\Sigma(X_{1},X_{2})=\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{S}_{0}[\Psi]}{\delta\Psi(X_{1})\delta\Psi(X_{2})}-\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma[\Psi]}{\delta\Psi(X_{1})\delta\Psi(X_{2})}=\Delta^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})-G^{-1}(X_{1},X_{2})\,.

(87)

One can show that by its definition (
83
),
\Gamma[\Psi]
only contains 1PI diagrams
8
8

8

This can, for example, be shown by considering the equations of motion for the full correlation functions, i. e. the respective
Dyson-Schwinger
equations, relating lower-order full correlators to higher-order full correlators.
, i. e. diagrams that cannot be separated by cutting one internal line. Consequently, the self-energy
\Sigma
only consists of 1PI diagrams with two amputated external legs. Equation (
87
) can formally be solved by multiplication with
G
and
\Delta
respectively, and leads to the
Dyson equation
,

G(X_{1},X_{2})=\Delta(X_{1},X_{2})+\int_{X_{3},X_{4}}\Delta(X_{1},X_{3})\cdot\Sigma(X_{3},X_{4})\cdot G(X_{4},X_{2})\,,

(88)

which iterates all 1PI diagrams by connecting them through
\Delta
. Equation (
88
) is a matrix integral equation. Its respective component equations read

\begin{split}G_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})=&\,\,\Delta_{ff}(X_{1},X_{2})+\int_{X_{3},X_{4}}\Delta_{ff}(X_{1},X_{3})\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{3},X_{4})G_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{4},X_{2})\\
&+\int_{X_{3},X_{4}}\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{3})\bigg[\Sigma_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{3},X_{4})G_{ff}(X_{4},X_{2})+\Sigma_{ff}(X_{3},X_{4})G_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{4},X_{2})\bigg]\,,\\[8.61108pt]
G_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})=&\,\,\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})+\int_{X_{3},X_{4}}\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{3})\Sigma_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{3},X_{4})G_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{4},X_{2})\,,\\[8.61108pt]
G_{\mathcal{B}f}(X_{1},X_{2})=&\,\,G_{f\mathcal{B}}(X_{2},X_{1})\,,\end{split}

(89)

and represent coupled integral equations that have to be solved numerically. In the above equations we have used that
\Delta_{\mathcal{B\mathcal{B}}}=0
as well as
\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}=0
, which follows from the fact that all pure
\mathcal{B}
-cumulants vanish identically. This implies that also
G_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}=0
. Furthermore,
G_{f\mathcal{B}}
inherits the causal structure of
\Delta_{f\mathcal{B}}.

We can now turn to the respective diagrams that contribute to the self-energy. Therefore note, that the interaction part of the action (
51
) consists of an infinite tower of
k
-point self-interactions. Thus, to one-loop order there exist two different topologies of diagrams that contribute to the self energy, given by

\displaystyle\hbox to63.6pt{\vbox to32.3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.7982pt\lower-16.1491pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.3}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.3} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.3}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.3} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -43.31 0 L -21.65 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -21.65 0 M -20.13 0 C -20.13 0.84 -20.81 1.52 -21.65 1.52 C -22.49 1.52 -23.18 0.84 -23.18 0 C -23.18 -0.84 -22.49 -1.52 -21.65 -1.52 C -20.81 -1.52 -20.13 -0.84 -20.13 0 Z M -21.65 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 21.65 0 M 23.18 0 C 23.18 0.84 22.49 1.52 21.65 1.52 C 20.81 1.52 20.13 0.84 20.13 0 C 20.13 -0.84 20.81 -1.52 21.65 -1.52 C 22.49 -1.52 23.18 -0.84 23.18 0 Z M 21.65 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

coming from the three- and four-point self-interactions. Indeed, the highest vertex contributing to the self-energy at
s
-loop order is an
2s+2
-point self-interaction, as it connects all propagator lines to the same vertex yielding generalizations of the diagram on the right in (
90
). Having determined the possible topologies at a given loop-level, we can now construct all contributing diagrams by inserting the respective vertices appearing in the action (
51
) and connecting them with the respective propagator. For our one-loop example, the three- and four point vertices are
G^{(0)}_{fff}\,,G^{(0)}_{ff\mathcal{B}}\,,G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}\,,G^{(0)}_{fff\mathcal{B}}\,,G^{(0)}_{ff\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}\,,G^{(0)}_{f\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}
. Keeping the above Feynman rules in mind, the one-loop macroscopic self-energy is given by the following set of diagrams

G^{(0)}_{\rho}
. In our case of interest however, we focus on a homogeneous setting, where those contributions identically vanish, as discussed in the next section.

(92)

To illustrate how the symmetry factors appearing in (
91
) and (
92
) are determined, let us consider the first diagram in (
91
):
First, there are
2\times 2=4
ways to attach the
\Delta_{ff}
propagators to the vertices. This number must be divided by the symmetry factors associated with the
G_{f\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}
cumulants at each vertex and the second-order Taylor expansion of the exponential. Specifically, each vertex contributes a factor of
\frac{1}{2!}
from the cumulants, and the second-order expansion contributes an additional factor of
\frac{1}{2!}
. Thus, the overall prefactor is
\frac{4}{(2!)^{3}}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Finally, the analytical expression for the first diagram reads
